Geometric interpretation of Lemma
21
involving the generalization error and relative entropies. Note that
\int D\left(P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|P_{Z}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=I\left(P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}};P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)
.

VII
Algorithm-Driven Gaps

The purpose of this section is to provide explicit expressions for the algorithm-driven gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
in (
20
) for an arbitrary dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
and two arbitrary probability measures
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
in
\triangle\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
.
In particular, these expressions are in terms of the Gibbs probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
33
), which represents a Gibbs algorithm.
For the ease of presentation, the analysis of
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
is performed using the fact that
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)=\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{2},P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
. Thus, the focus is on the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
, for an arbitrary measure
P\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
.

VII-A

Deviations from the Gibbs Measure

The variation or
sensitivity
of the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
9
) to deviations from the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
33
) to an alternative probability measure
P
is

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right),

(184)

where the functional
\mathsf{G}
is defined in (
20
).
The following theorem introduces an explicit expression for
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
.

Lemma 22
(Theorem
1
in
[
56
]
)

The probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
33
) satisfies for all
P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

(185)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\left(D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)+D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-D\left(P\|Q\right)\right),

where the functional
\mathsf{G}
is defined in (
20
).

An interesting observation from Lemma
22
is that when the reference measure
Q
is a probability measure, it holds that
D\left(P\|Q\right)~\geqslant~0

[
9
, Theorem
1
]
, which leads to the following corollary of Lemma
22
.

Corollary 23

If
Q
is a probability measure, then the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
184
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\lambda\left(D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)+D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\right),

(186)

with equality if
D\left(P\|Q\right)=0
.

Also from Lemma
22
, under the assumption that
Q
is a probability measure, it follows that the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
184
) is nonnegative for all measures
P
within a neighborhood of
Q
, as suggested in the following corollary.

Corollary 24

For all
P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
, such that
D\left(P\|Q\right)\leqslant D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)
, with
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
33
), the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
184
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle 0

(187)

with equality if
D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=0
.

VII-B

Priors and Posteriors

From Lemma
22
, under the assumption that
Q
is a probability measure, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(Q\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\left(D\left(Q\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)\right),

where the right-hand side is a symmetrized Kullback-Leibler divergence, also known as Jeffreys divergence
[
77
]
, between the measures
Q
and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
.
More importantly, when
Q
is a probability measure, it follows from
[
9
, Theorem
1
]
that
D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)\geqslant 0
and
D\left(Q\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\geqslant 0
, which reveals the fact that the expected empirical risk induced by the Gibbs probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is smaller than or equal to the expected empirical risk induced by the reference measure
Q
. This observation is formalized by the following corollary of Lemma
22
.

Corollary 25

If
Q
is a probability measure, then the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
33
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\leqslant\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(Q\right),

(188)

where the function
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
9
).

Note that the reference measure
Q
in Corollary
25
can be chosen independently of the dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
. From this perspective, the measure
Q
can be interpreted as a prior on the models, while the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
can be interpreted as a posterior once the prior
Q
is confronted with the training dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
.

VII-C

A Geometric Interpretation

In Lemma
22
, note that if
Q
is a probability measure, then it holds from
[
9
, Theorem
1
]
that
D\left(P\|Q\right)\geqslant 0
,
D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\geqslant 0
, and
D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)\geqslant 0
. Thus, from (
184
), it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+\lambda D\left(P\|Q\right)

(189)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)+\lambda D\left(P\|Q\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+\lambda D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

(190)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right),

(191)

and moreover,

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+\lambda D\left(P\|Q\right)

(192)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\left(D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)+D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\lambda D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right),

(193)

where the inequality in (
190
) follows from (
33
); and the equality in (
192
) follows from Lemma
22
.

Hence, under the assumption that the measure
Q
is a probability measure, the equality in (
185
) can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+D\left(P\|Q\right)}\right)^{2}

(194)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)}\right)^{2}+\left(\sqrt{D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}\right)^{2},

which together with (
192
) and (
193
) imply that a right-angled triangle can be constructed such that the hypotenuse exhibits length
\sqrt{\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+D\left(P\|Q\right)}
and the short sides exhibit lengths

\sqrt{D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)}
and
\sqrt{D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}
, respectively.
Figure
6
shows this interpretation of the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
184
).

\sqrt{D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)}
\sqrt{D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}
\sqrt{\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+D\left(P\|Q\right)}

Fig. 6:
Geometric interpretation of the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P,P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
184
).

VII-D

Sensitivity from/to Arbitrary Deviations

Given two probability measures
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, both in
\triangle\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
, and a dataset
\boldsymbol{z}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
, the focus is on the variation of the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
9
) from
P_{2}
to
P_{1}
. In terms of the functional
\mathsf{G}
in (
20
), such a variation is
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
.
The following theorem provides an exact expression for such a variation.

Theorem 26

If the probability measures
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are both absolutely continuous with respect to the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
33
), then the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
in (
20
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\bigg(D\left(P_{1}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-D\left(P_{2}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

(195)

\displaystyle+D\left(P_{2}\|Q\right)-D\left(P_{1}\|Q\right)\bigg),

where the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
33
).

Proof:

The proof follows from Lemma
22
and by noticing that
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)=\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{2},P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
.
∎

In Theorem
26
, as long as the probability measures
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are both absolutely continuous with respect to the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
, the reference measure
Q
can be arbitrarily chosen. Similarly, the parameter
\lambda
can be arbitrarily chosen within the set

\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
31
). Some notions of optimality for the choice of
\lambda
have been studied in
[
78
]
.
This flexibility in the choice of these parameters is essentially because only the right-hand side of (
195
) depends on them.
Another interesting observation is that none of the terms in the right-hand side of (
195
) depends simultaneously on both
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.
These observations highlight the significant flexibility of the expression in (
195
) to construct closed-form expressions for the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
.

VII-E

Choice of the Reference Measure

The choice of
Q
in (
195
) is subject to the constraint that both probability measures
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
must be absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
.
From this perspective, the measure
Q
can be seen as a free parameter, which can be strategically chosen.

VII-E
1
Probability Measures

Two choices of
Q
, as a probability measure, for which the expression in the right-hand side of (
195
) significantly simplifies are
Q=P_{1}
and
Q=P_{2}
, which leads to the following corollary of Theorem
26
.

Corollary 27

If
P_{1}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{2}
, then
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
in (
195
) satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)

(196)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\bigg(D\left(P_{1}\|P^{\left(P_{2},\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-D\left(P_{1}\|P_{2}\right)-D\left(P_{2}\|P^{\left(P_{2},\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\bigg).

Alternatively, if
P_{2}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{1}
then

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)

(197)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\bigg(D\left(P_{1}\|P^{\left(P_{1},\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+D\left(P_{2}\|P_{1}\right)-D\left(P_{2}\|P^{\left(P_{1},\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\bigg),

where for all
i\in\{1,2\}
, the probability measure
P^{\left(P_{i},\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
satisfies (
33
) under the assumption that
Q=P_{i}
.

In the case in which none of the measures is absolutely continuous with respect to its counterpart, choosing
Q
as a convex combination of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
guarantees an explicit expression for
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
in (
195
). The following corollary formalizes this observation.

Corollary 28

For all
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right)
,
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
in (
195
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)

(198)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\bigg(D\left(P_{1}\|P^{\left(\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2},\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-D\left(P_{2}\|P^{\left(\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2},\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle+D\left(P_{2}\|\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2}\right)-D\left(P_{1}\|\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2}\right)\bigg),

where the probability measure
P^{\left(\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2},\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
satisfies (
33
) under the assumption that
Q=\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2}
.

The measure
Q
in (
33
) can also be chosen as a
\sigma
-finite measure, e.g., the counting measure or the Lebesgue measure.

VII-E
2
The Counting Measure

Assume for instance that the set of models
\mathcal{M}
is countable and the reference measure
Q
is the counting measure.
Under this assumption, any probability measure
P\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the counting measure
Q
.
The Radon-Nikodym derivative of
P
with respect to
Q
is the probability mass function of
P
, denoted by
p:\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]
. Interestingly, under the current assumption,
D\left(P\|Q\right)
is equal to negative Shannon’s discrete entropy of
P
. That is,
D\left(P\|Q\right)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}}p(\boldsymbol{\theta})\log\left(p(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\triangleq-H\left(P\right)
.
This observation leads to the following corollary of Theorem
26
.

Corollary 29

Let the set
\mathcal{M}
be countable and let
Q
in (
33
) be the corresponding counting measure. Then, the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
in (
20
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)

(199)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\bigg(D\left(P_{1}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-D\left(P_{2}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-H\left(P_{2}\right)+H\left(P_{1}\right)\bigg),

where the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
33
); and the terms
H\left(P_{1}\right)
and
H\left(P_{2}\right)
represent Shannon’s discrete entropy of the probability measures
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, respectively.

From
[
9
, Theorem
3
]
, it follows that the probability mass function of the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
199
), denoted
p_{\lambda,\boldsymbol{z}}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]
, satisfies

\displaystyle p_{\lambda,\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(200)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)}{\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)},

(201)

where the function
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
is in (
33
).

An important observation from Corollary
29
is that for all
i\in\{1,2\}
, while the term
D\left(P_{i}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
is convex in
P_{i}
, the term
H\left(P_{i}\right)
is concave in
P_{i}

[
9
, Theorem
2
]
. Interestingly, the term
H(P_{i})
is independent of the dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
, the choice of the loss function
\ell
in (
6
), and the regularization factor
\lambda
. Moreover, while the term
D\left(P_{i}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
is minimized when
P_{i}=P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, the term
H(P_{i})
is minimized when
P_{i}
is concentrated on any singleton of
\mathcal{M}
. This shows the trade-off arising in model selection between randomly choosing the models by sampling the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and choosing a model with probability one.

VII-E
3
The Lebesgue Measure

Assume that the set
\mathcal{M}
is a subset of
\mathds{R}^{d}
, and the reference measure
Q
is the corresponding Lebesgue measure.
Under these assumptions, the Radon-Nikodym derivative of a given absolutely continuous probability measure
P
with respect to
Q
is the probability density function of
P
, denoted by
p:\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]
. Under the current assumption,
D\left(P\|Q\right)
is equal to negative Shannon’s differential entropy of
P
. That is,
D\left(P\|Q\right)=\int p(\boldsymbol{\theta})\log\left(p(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\theta}\triangleq-h(P)
.
This observation leads to the following corollary of Theorem
26
.

Corollary 30

Let the set
\mathcal{M}
be a subset of
\mathds{R}^{d}
, and let
Q
in (
33
) be the corresponding Lebesgue measure. Then, the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
in (
20
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P_{1},P_{2}\right)

(202)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\bigg(D\left(P_{1}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-D\left(P_{2}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-h\left(P_{2}\right)+h\left(P_{1}\right)\bigg),

where the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
33
); and the terms
h\left(P_{1}\right)
and
h\left(P_{2}\right)
represent Shannon’s differential entropy of the probability measures
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, respectively.

From
[
9
, Theorem
3
]
, it follows that the probability density function of the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
202
), denoted
p_{\lambda,\boldsymbol{z}}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]
, satisfies

\displaystyle p_{\lambda,\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(203)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)}{\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}},

(204)

where the function
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
is in (
33
).
Despite the fact that for all
i\in\{1,2\}
, the convexity of
D\left(P_{i}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
and the concavity of
h\left(P_{i}\right)
with respect to
P_{i}
hold as in the previous case, these terms do not lead to useful interpretations as, for instance, the differential entropy
h\left(P_{i}\right)
might be negative and unboundedly increase or decrease.

VIII
Data-Driven Gaps

The purpose of this section is to provide explicit expressions for the data-driven gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},Q_{1},Q_{2}\right)
in (
21
) for an arbitrary model
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
and two arbitrary probability measures
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
in
\triangle\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)
. Interestingly, these expressions are in terms of the WCDG probability measure
P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}~
in (
51
).

VIII-A

Deviations from the WCDG probability measure

The variation or
sensitivity
of the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in (
8
) to deviations from the WCDG probability measure
P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}~
in (
51
) to an alternative probability measure
P\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)
is

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right),

(205)

where the functional
\mathsf{G}
is defined in (
21
). The following lemma introduces an explicit expression for
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)
.

Lemma 31
(
[
53
, Theorem 6]
)

The probability measure
P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}~
in (
51
) satisfies, for all
P\in\Delta_{P_{S}}\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta\left(D\left(P\|P_{S}\right)-D\left(P\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)-D\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\|P_{S}\right)\right),

(206)

where the functional
\mathsf{G}
is defined in (
21
).

VIII-B

Sensitivity from/to Arbitrary Deviations

Given two probability measures
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, both in
\triangle\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)
, and a model
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
, the focus is on the variation of the function
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in (
8
) from
P_{2}
to
P_{1}
. In terms of the functional
\mathsf{G}
in (
21
), such a variation is
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P_{1},P_{2}\right)
. The following theorem provides an exact expression for such a variation.

Theorem 32
(
[
53
, Theorem
8
]
)

For all
P_{1}\in\Delta_{P_{S}}\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)
and
P_{2}\in\Delta_{P_{S}}\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)
, and for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(\boldsymbol{\theta},P_{1},P_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\Bigl(D\left(P_{2}\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)-D\left(P_{1}\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)

(207)

\displaystyle-D\left(P_{2}\|P_{S}\right)+D\left(P_{1}\|P_{S}\right)\Bigl),

where the functional
\mathsf{G}
is defined in (
21
); and the parameter
\beta
, the model
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, and the measures
P_{S}
and
P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}~
jointly satisfy (
51
).

VIII-C

Choice of the Reference Measure

The choice of the reference measure
P_{S}
in (
207
) is subject to the constraint that both probability measures
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
must be absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{S}
. Despite this constraint, the reference measure
P_{S}
can be seen as a free parameter, which can be strategically chosen.
Two choices of
P_{S}
, for which the expression in the right-hand side of (
207
) significantly simplifies are
P_{S}=P_{1}
and
P_{S}=P_{2}
, which leads to the following corollary of Theorem
32
.

Corollary 33
(
[
57
, Corollary 3]
)

If
P_{1}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{2}
, then the gap
\mathsf{G}(\boldsymbol{\theta},P_{1},P_{2})
in (
21
) satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(\boldsymbol{\theta},P_{1},P_{2})

(208)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\left(D\left(P_{2}\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{2},\beta\right)}\right)-D\left(P_{1}\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{2},\beta\right)}\right)+D\left(P_{1}\|P_{2}\right)\right).

Alternatively, if
P_{2}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{1}
then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(\boldsymbol{\theta},P_{1},P_{2})

(209)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\left(D\left(P_{2}\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{1},\beta\right)}\right)-D\left(P_{1}\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{1},\beta\right)}\right)-D\left(P_{2}\|P_{1}\right)\right),

where for all
i\in\{1,2\}
, the probability measure
P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{i},\beta\right)}
satisfies (
51
) under the assumption that
P_{S}=P_{i}
.

Interestingly, absolute continuity of
P_{1}
with respect to
P_{2}
or
P_{2}
with respect to
P_{1}
is not necessary for obtaining an expression for the gap
\mathsf{G}(\boldsymbol{\theta},P_{1},P_{2})
in (
21
). In the case in which none of the measures is absolutely continuous with respect to its counterpart, choosing
P_{S}
as a convex combination of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
always guarantees an explicit expression for the gap
\mathsf{G}(\boldsymbol{\theta},P_{1},P_{2})
.
The following corollary formalizes this observation.

Corollary 34

For all
P_{1}\in\Delta_{P_{S}}\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)
and
P_{2}\in\Delta_{P_{S}}\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)
, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
, and for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(\boldsymbol{\theta},P_{1},P_{2})

(210)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\Bigl(D\left(P_{2}\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2},\beta\right)}\right)-D\left(P_{1}\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2},\beta\right)}\right)

\displaystyle-D\left(P_{2}\|\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2}\right)+D\left(P_{1}\|\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2}\right)\Bigl),

where the functional
\mathsf{G}
is defined in (
21
); and the parameter
\beta
, the model
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, and the measures
\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2}
and
P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2},\beta\right)}~
jointly satisfy (
51
).

VIII-D

A Geometric Interpretation

\sqrt{D\left(P\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)}
\sqrt{D\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\|P_{S}\right)}
\sqrt{D\left(P\|P_{S}\right)-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)}

Fig. 7:
Geometric interpretation of the gap
\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)
in (
206
).

In Lemma
31
, the reference measure
P_{S}
is a probability measure. Then, from
[
9
, Theorem
1
]
, it follows that
D\left(P\|P_{S}\right)\geq~0,D\left(P\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)\geq~0
and
D\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\|P_{S}\right)\geq~0
.
This observation together with (
206
) lead to the following inequality:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle D\left(P\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)+D\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\|P_{S}\right)

(211)

\displaystyle=D\left(P\|P_{S}\right)-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\beta D\left(P\|P_{S}\right)-\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)

(212)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P\right)-\beta D\left(P\|P_{S}\right)\right)+\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle-\left(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)-\beta D\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\|P_{S}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta D\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\|P_{S}\right),

(213)

where the equality in (
212
) follows from
[
53
, Lemma
3
]
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\beta D\left(P\|P_{S}\right)-\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)

(214)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta D\left(P\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)+\beta D\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\|P_{S}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\beta D\left(P\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right),

(215)

where (
214
) follows from Lemma
31
.

From these observations, the equality in (
206
) can be written as follows:

\displaystyle{\left(\sqrt{D\left(P\|P_{S}\right)-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)}\right)}^{2}

(216)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\left(\sqrt{D\left(P\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)}\right)}^{2}+{\left(\sqrt{D\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\|P_{S}\right)}\right)}^{2}.

The equality in (
216
) together with the inequalities in (
213
) and (
215
) imply that a right-angled triangle can be constructed such that the hypotenuse exhibits length
\sqrt{D\left(P\|P_{S}\right)-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathsf{G}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},P,P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)}
and the short sides exhibit lengths
\sqrt{D\left(P\|P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\right)}
and
\sqrt{D\left(P_{\hat{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\left(P_{S},\beta\right)}\|P_{S}\right)}
, respectively. Figure
7
shows this geometric interpretation.

IX
Conclusions and Final Remarks

In this work, closed-form expressions for the generalization error of supervised machine learning algorithms have been derived in terms of information-theoretic measures. Two distinct classes of expressions are identified. The first class encompasses formulations involving the Gibbs algorithm, as defined by the Gibbs probability measure in Definition
5
. The second class pertains to expressions incorporating the WCDG probability measure, defined in Definition
6
.
While the former represents a more general framework, as it imposes no specific conditions on the probability distribution of datasets, both classes offer unique and complementary insights. These insights establish connections between the generalization error and concepts such as hypothesis testing, information measures, and Euclidean geometry. However, these connections have been only briefly explored in this work, and their full potential to enhance the understanding of generalization error remains an open avenue for further research.

It is important to note that the derived expressions do not significantly simplify the numerical computation of the generalization error. This is unsurprising, as the primary goal of this study is to provide a deeper conceptual understanding of the generalization error from an information-theoretic perspective. Specifically, the established connections between the generalization error, the Gibbs algorithm, and the WCDG probability measure represent a significant advancement in this goal.
Concerning the connection to the Gibbs algorithm, the expression that best unveils the relevance of these findings is the one in Theorem
12
. Note that such expression implies that the generalization error of an arbitrary machine learning algorithm can be understood as a comparison, via relative entropy, of all possible instances of the algorithm under study with all possible instances of the Gibbs algorithm in two scenarios:
(a)
Both algorithms are trained upon the same training datasets drawn from the same probability distribution; and
(b)
Both algorithms are trained upon training datasets independently drawn from the same probability distribution.
Alternatively, concerning the connection to the WCDG probability measure, the expression that best unveils the relevance of these findings is the one in Theorem
20
. The expression therein implies that the generalization error of a given machine learning algorithm consists of a comparison, via relative entropy, of all possible likelihoods on the data points induced by the algorithm under study; and the WCDG probability measure in two scenarios:
(a)
both probability measures, e.g., likelihood and WCDG probability measures, are conditioned on the same model drawn from the same probability distribution; and
(b)
both probability measures are respectively conditioned upon models independently drawn from the same probability distribution.

Another important finding, e.g., from Theorem
10
, is that the generalization error is the sum of two quantities:
(a)
A first quantity that characterizes the dependence of the models on the training data via the mutual and lautum information; and
(b)
a second quantity that characterizes a statistical distance between the algorithm under study and a Gibbs algorithm.
While the first quantity
(a)
is always nonnegative, the second quantity
(b)
might be negative, zero, or positive. Clearly, a variation in any of these quantities,
(a)
or
(b)
, implies a variation in the other, and thus, they cannot be separately optimized for algorithm design.
Nonetheless, these quantities place Gibbs algorithms as natural references to evaluate the generalization capabilities of supervised machine learning algorithms.

Appendix A
Statistical Properties of Algorithms

An algorithm
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M}|\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\right)
and a probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\in\triangle\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\right)
determine two unique probability measures in
\triangle\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\times\mathcal{M}\right)
and
\triangle\left(\mathcal{M}\times\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\right)
, respectively. Such probability measures are respectively denoted by
P_{\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
and
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{Z}}}
and for all measurable sets
\mathcal{A}\subset\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\times\mathcal{M}
, it follows that

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right),

(217)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is the section of the set
\mathcal{A}
determined by
\boldsymbol{z}
, namely,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\triangleq\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta})\in\mathcal{A}\right\}.

(218)

Alternatively, for all measurable sets
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{M}\times\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
, it follows that

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right),

(219)

where
\mathcal{B}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is the section of the set
\mathcal{B}
determined by
\boldsymbol{z}
, namely,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\triangleq\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z})\in\mathcal{B}\right\}.

(220)

If the sets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are of the form
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{C}
, for some
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
and
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
, then, it holds that

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}\right)=\int_{\mathcal{C}}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{D}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)=P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{C}\right).

(221)

In particular, if
\mathcal{C}=\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
in (
221
), then, it follows that

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\times\mathcal{D}\right)=P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{D}\times\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\right)=P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\mathcal{D}\right),

(222)

where the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
also satisfies (
28
).

Alternatively, if
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{M}
in (
221
), then, it holds that

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{M}\right)=P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{C}\right)=P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{C}\right).

(223)

Hence, in the following, the measures
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
in (
222
) and
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
in (
223
) are said to be the marginals of the joint probability measures
P_{\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
in (
217
) and
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}
in (
219
).

The instance
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
of the algorithm
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}
, for some dataset
\boldsymbol{z}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
, can be interpreted as the posterior probability measure of the prior
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
after the observation of the dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
.
Let the conditional probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\in\triangle\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}|\mathcal{M}\right)
be such that for all measurable sets
\mathcal{C}\subset\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
,

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

(224)

with
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
in (
222
) and
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
in
(
223
).
Such a conditional probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
exists and is unique almost surely with respect to
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}

[
63
, Theorem
5.3.1
]
.
The probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
can be interpreted as the likelihood (on the datasets) induced by the posterior
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}
and the ground-truth probability distribution of the datasets
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
.
The joint probability measures
P_{\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
and
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}
can be described via the conditional probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}
and the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
as in (
217
) and in (
219
); or via the conditional probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
and the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
.
More specifically, for all measurable sets
\mathcal{A}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\times\mathcal{M}
, it follows that

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

(225)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is the section of the set
\mathcal{A}
determined by
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, namely,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\left\{\boldsymbol{z}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}:(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta})\in\mathcal{A}\right\}.

(226)

Alternatively, for all measurable sets
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{M}\times\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
, it follows that

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

(227)

where the section
\mathcal{B}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
of the set
\mathcal{B}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\left\{\boldsymbol{z}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}:(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z})\in\mathcal{B}\right\}.

(228)

For all measurable sets
\mathcal{A}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\times\mathcal{M}
, let the set
\hat{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{M}\times\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
be

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z})\in\mathcal{M}\times\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}:(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta})\in\mathcal{A}\right\},

(229)

then, from (
225
) and (
227
), it holds that

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\hat{\mathcal{A}}\right).

(230)

The following lemmas, Lemma
35
and Lemma
36
, present some properties of the probability measures mentioned above, which are known, but given their central roles in this work, are presented and proved using the current notation.

Lemma 35
(Theorem 11 in
[
58
]
)

Consider the conditional probability measures
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}
and
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
; and the probability measures
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
and
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
that jointly satisfy (

) and (

).
Assume that:

(a)

For all

, the probability measure

is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
; and

(b)

For all

, the probability measure

.

Then, for all
\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\times\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

(231)

almost surely with respect to the product measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\in\triangle\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\times\mathcal{M}\right)
.

Lemma 36
(Theorem 12 in
[

]
)

Consider the conditional probability measures

Lemma 37

Consider the conditional probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
and the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
, both in (

.

Proof:

For all measurable sets

and for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

(233)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(234)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{A}_{z}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{Z}\left(z\right),

(235)

where the equality in (

(b)
and
[

, Theorem 2]
;
and the equality in (

) follows from Assumption
(a)
,
where the set

is the section of the set
\mathcal{A}
determined by
z\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{z}=\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n-1}:\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\in\mathcal{A}\right\},

(236)

and the measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}}
is a product distribution in
\triangle\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n-1}\right)
formed by
P_{Z}
. That is, the product measure

is identical to the measure

in (

).

Given a measurable set
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, consider the set
\mathcal{A}
that satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{A}^{(2)}\times\mathcal{A}^{(3)}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}^{(n)},

(237)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(2)}=\mathcal{A}^{(3)}=\cdots=\mathcal{A}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}.

(238)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n-1}.

(239)

Using such a fact, it follows that for the sets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in (

,

\displaystyle P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

(240)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{A}_{z}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{Z}\left(z\right)

(241)

\displaystyle\iint\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{Z}\left(z\right),

, Lemma

the equality in (

Hence, given that the set

, imply that the probability measure

.
Moreover, the Radon-Nikodym derivative of

with respect to

, is

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{Z}}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right),

(243)

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 38

Consider the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
and the conditional probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
, both in (
224
). Assume that:

(a)

The probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
satisfies (
22
) for some
P_{Z}\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)
; and

(b)

For all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
, the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.

Then, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
, the probability measure
P_{Z}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in (
40
).

Proof:

The proof follows along the lines of the proof of Lemma
37
.
For all measurable sets
\mathcal{A}\subset\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
and for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

(244)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(245)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{A}_{z}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}|Z_{1}=z,\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{Z_{1}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(z\right)

(246)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{A}_{z}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}|Z_{1}=z,\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(z\right),

(247)

where the equality in (
245
) follows from Assumption
(b)
and
[
60
, Theorem
2.2.3
]
; and
in the equality (
246
), the set
\mathcal{A}_{z}
is defined in (
236
) and the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}|Z_{1}=z,\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\triangle\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n-1}\right)
is the one that satisfies for all measurable subsets
\mathcal{C}
of
\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n-1}
,

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)\times\mathcal{C}\right)

(248)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}|Z_{1}=z,\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{Z_{1}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(z\right),

where the measure
P_{Z_{1}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is the marginal defined in (
38
); and finally, the equality (
247
) follows from Assumption
(a)
and Lemma
5
.

The proof continues by noticing that for a given measurable subset
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, the set
\mathcal{A}
in (
237
) satisfies for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle P_{Z}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)=P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

(249)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{A}_{z}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}|Z_{1}=z,\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(z\right),

(250)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}|Z_{1}=z,\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(z\right),

(251)

where the equality in (
249
) follows from Assumption
(a)
;
the equality in (
250
) follows from (
247
); and
the equality in (
251
) follows from (
239
).
Hence, given that the set
\mathcal{B}
can be any measurable subset of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, the equality in (
251
), together with
[
60
, Theorem
2.2.3
]
, imply that the probability measure
P_{Z}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.
Moreover, the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
P_{Z}
with respect to
P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, denoted by
\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{Z}}{\mathrm{d}P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
, is

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{Z}}{\mathrm{d}P_{Z|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left(z,\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{-1}|Z_{1}=z,\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right),

(252)

which completes the proof.
∎

and Lemma

lead to the following corollary.

Corollary 39

Consider the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
and the conditional probability measure

, both in (

). Assume that:

(a)

) for some

(b)

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
, the probability measures
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
and
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}
are mutually absolutely continuous.

Then, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
, the probability measures
P_{Z}
and

in (

) are mutually absolutely continuous.

This appendix ends by stating the following property of the functionals
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in (

) and

in (

).

Lemma 40

Consider the conditional probability measures
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}
and
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
; and the probability measures
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
and
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
that jointly satisfy (
28
) and (
224
).
Then, the functionals
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in (
8
) and
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
9
), satisfy

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right).

(253)

Consider also the following assumptions:

(a)

For all
\boldsymbol{z}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
, the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
; and

(b)

For all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
, the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
.

Then, the functionals
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
also satisfy:

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right).

(254)

Proof:

The proof of (
253
) follows from observing that

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(255)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(256)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right).

(257)

where the equality in (
255
) follows from (
9
);
the equality in (
256
) follows by exchanging the order of the integrals
[
63
, Theorem
2.6.6
]
;
and finally, the equality in (
257
) follows from (
8
).
This completes the proof of (

).

The proof of (
254
) follows from observing that

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(258)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(259)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(260)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(261)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(262)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}|\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

(263)

where the equality in (

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
3

The proof is essentially algebraic and follows from Definition
4
. More specifically,

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G}}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}},P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)

(264)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\left(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\int\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right),

(265)

where the equality in (

) follows from (
9
).
The proof follows by noticing that

\displaystyle\iint\int\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(266)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\left(\int\mathsf{L}\left(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle\iint\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(268)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\left(\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(269)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(270)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right),

(271)

where the equality in (

) follows by exchanging the order of the integrals
[

, Theorem
2.6.6
]
;

) follows from (
8
);

Plugging the equality (

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G}}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}},P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)

(272)

\displaystyle=

which completes the proof.

Appendix C

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
9

The proof follows along the lines of the proof of
[
58
, Theorem 18]
.
From Theorem
8
, it follows that the generalization error
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G}}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}},P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)
in (
11
) satisfies:

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G}}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}},P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)

(295)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int_{\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda\int_{\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(296)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\lambda\gamma P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}\right)+\lambda\underline{\gamma}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}\right)

(297)

\displaystyle-\lambda\bar{\gamma}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}\right)-\lambda\gamma P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}\right)

(298)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\left(\underline{\gamma}-\gamma\right)P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}\right)+\lambda\left(\gamma-\bar{\gamma}\right)P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}\right),

(299)

where the inequality in (
298
) follows from the definition of the set
\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}
in (
68
); and the extrema in (
71
) and (
72
). Finally,
the equality in (
299
) follows from (
69
) and (
70
).
This completes the proof of the lower bound.

The proof of the upper bound follows a similar argument. From the equality in (
296
), the following holds,

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G}}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}},P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)

(300)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\lambda\bar{\gamma}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}\right)+\lambda\gamma P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}\right)

\displaystyle-\lambda\gamma P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}\right)-\lambda\underline{\gamma}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\left(\bar{\gamma}-\gamma\right)P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}\right)+\lambda\left(\gamma-\underline{\gamma}\right)P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}\right),

(301)

where the inequality in (
300
) follows from the definition of the set
\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}
in (
68
); and the extrema in (
71
) and (
72
). Finally,
the equality in (
301
) follows from (
69
) and (
70
).
This completes the proof of the upper bound.

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
11

The proof follows along the lines of the proof of
[
58
, Theorem 18]
.
From Theorem
10
, it follows that the difference
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G}}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}},P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)-\lambda\left(I\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}};P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)+L\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}};P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\right)
satisfies:

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G}}}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}},P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)-\lambda\left(I\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}};P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)+L\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}};P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\right)

(303)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(304)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\lambda\gamma P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}\right)+\lambda\underline{\gamma}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}\right)

(305)

\displaystyle-\lambda\bar{\gamma}P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}\right)-\lambda\gamma P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}\right)

(306)

\displaystyle\lambda\left(\underline{\gamma}-\gamma\right)P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{c}}\right)+\lambda\left(\gamma-\bar{\gamma}\right)P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}\right),

where the inequality in (

) follows from the definition of the set
\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}

) and (

). Finally,
